PL/pgSQL depends on this so that it can share the\n618| * same lexer. WORK WRAPPER WRITE\n724|\n725|\tXML_P XMLATTRIBUTES XMLCONCAT XMLELEMENT XMLEXISTS XMLFOREST XMLNAMESPACES\n726|\tXMLPARSE XMLPI XMLROOT XMLSERIALIZE XMLTABLE\n727|\n728|\tYEAR_P YES_P\n729|\n730|\tZONE\n731|\n732|/*\n733| * The grammar thinks these are keywords, but they are not in the kwlist.h\n734| * list and so can never be entered directly.  The filter in parser.c\n735| * creates these tokens when required (based on looking one token ahead).\n736| *\n737| * NOT_LA exists so that productions such as NOT LIKE can be given the same\n738| * precedence as LIKE; otherwise they'd effectively have the same precedence\n739| * as NOT, at least with respect to their left-hand subexpression.\n740| * NULLS_LA and WITH_LA are needed to make the grammar LALR(1).\n741| */\n742|%token\t\tNOT_LA NULLS_LA WITH_LA\n743|\n744|/*\n745| * The grammar likewise thinks these tokens are keywords, but they are never\n746| * generated by the scanner.  Rather, they can be injected by parser.c as\n747| * the initial token of the string (using the lookahead-token mechanism\n748| * implemented there).  This provides a way to tell the grammar to parse\n749| * something other than the usual list of SQL commands.\n750| */\n751|%token\t\tMODE_TYPE_NAME\n752|%token\t\tMODE_PLPGSQL_EXPR\n753|%token\t\tMODE_PLPGSQL_ASSIGN1\n754|%token\t\tMODE_PLPGSQL_ASSIGN2\n755|%token\t\tMODE_PLPGSQL_ASSIGN3\n756|\n757|\n758|/* Precedence: lowest to highest */\n759|%nonassoc\tSET\t\t\t\t/* see relation_expr_opt_alias */\n760|%left\t\tUNION EXCEPT\n761|%left\t\tINTERSECT\n762|%left\t\tOR\n763|%left\t\tAND\n764|%right\t\tNOT\n765|%nonassoc\tIS ISNULL NOTNULL\t/* IS sets precedence for IS NULL, etc */\n766|%nonassoc\t'<' '>' '=' LESS_EQUALS GREATER_EQUALS NOT_EQUALS\n767|%nonassoc\tBETWEEN IN_P LIKE ILIKE SIMILAR NOT_LA\n768|%nonassoc\tESCAPE\t\t\t/* ESCAPE must be just above LIKE/ILIKE/SIMILAR */\n769|/*\n770| * To support target_el without AS, it used to be necessary to assign IDENT an\n771| * explicit precedence just less than Op.  While that's not really necessary\n772| * since we removed postfix operators, it's still helpful to do so because\n773| * there are some other unreserved keywords that need precedence assignments.\n774| * If those keywords have the same precedence as IDENT then they clearly act\n775| * the same as non-keywords, reducing the risk of unwanted precedence effects.\n776| *\n777| * We need to do this for PARTITION, RANGE, ROWS, and GROUPS to support\n778| * opt_existing_window_name (see comment there).\n779| *\n780| * The frame_bound productions UNBOUNDED PRECEDING and UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING\n781| * are even messier: since UNBOUNDED is an unreserved keyword (per spec!),\n782| * there is no principled way to distinguish these from the productions\n783| * a_expr PRECEDING/FOLLOWING.  We hack this up by giving UNBOUNDED slightly\n784| * lower precedence than PRECEDING and FOLLOWING.  At present this doesn't\n785| * appear to cause UNBOUNDED to be treated differently from other unreserved\n786| * keywords anywhere else in the grammar, but it's definitely risky.  We can\n787| * blame any funny behavior of UNBOUNDED on the SQL standard, though.\n788| *\n789| * To support CUBE and ROLLUP in GROUP BY without reserving them, we give them\n790| * an explicit priority lower than '(', so that a rule with CUBE '(' will shift\n791| * rather than reducing a conflicting rule that takes CUBE as a function name.\n792| * Using the same precedence as IDENT seems right for the reasons given above.\n793| */\n794|%nonassoc\tUNBOUNDED\t\t/* ideally would have same precedence as IDENT */\n795|%nonassoc\tIDENT PARTITION RANGE ROWS GROUPS PRECEDING FOLLOWING CUBE ROLLUP\n796|%left\t\tOp OPERATOR\t\t/* multi-character ops and user-defined operators */\n797|%left\t\t'+' '-'\n798|%left\t\t'*' '/' '%'\n799|%left\t\t'^'\n800|/* Unary Operators */\n801|%left\t\tAT\t\t\t\t/* sets precedence for AT TIME ZONE */\n802|%left\t\tCOLLATE\n803|%right\t\tUMINUS\n804|%left\t\t'[' ']'\n805|%left\t\t'(' ')'\n806|%left\t\tTYPECAST\n807|%left\t\t'.'\n808|/*\n809| * These might seem to be low-precedence, but actually they are not part\n810| * of the arithmetic hierarchy at all in their use as JOIN operators.\n811| * We make them high-precedence to support their use as function names.\n812| * They wouldn't be given a precedence at all, were it not that we need\n813| * left-associativity among the JOIN rules themselves.\n814| */\n815|%left\t\tJOIN CROSS LEFT FULL RIGHT INNER_P NATURAL\n816|\n817|%%\n818|\n819|/*\n820| *\tThe target production for the whole parse.\n821| *\n822| * Ordinarily }\n11280|\t\t\t| cursor_options BINARY\t\t\t{ $$ = $1 | CURSOR_OPT_BINARY; }\n11281|\t\t\t| cursor_options ASENSITIVE\t\t{ $$ = $1 | CURSOR_OPT_ASENSITIVE; }\n11282|\t\t\t| cursor_options INSENSITIVE\t{ $$ = $1 | CURSOR_OPT_INSENSITIVE; }\n11283|\t\t;\n11284|\n11285|opt_hold: /* EMPTY */\t\t\t\t\t\t{ $$ = 0; }\n11286|\t\t\t| WITH HOLD\t\t\t\t\t\t{ $$ = CURSOR_OPT_HOLD; }\n11287|\t\t\t| WITHOUT HOLD\t\t\t\t\t{ $$ = 0; }\n11288|\t\t;\n11289|\n11290|/*****************************************************************************\n11291| *\n11292| *\t\tQUERY:\n11293| *\t\t\t\tSELECT STATEMENTS\n11294| *\n11295| *****************************************************************************/\n11296|\n11297|/* A complete SELECT statement looks like this.\n11298| *\n11299| * The rule returns either a single SelectStmt node or a tree of them,\n11300| * representing a set-operation tree.\n11301| *\n11302| * There is an ambiguity when a sub-SELECT is within an a_expr and there\n11303| * are excess parentheses: do the parentheses belong to the sub-SELECT or\n11304| * to the surrounding a_expr?  We don't really care, but bison wants to know.\n11305| * To resolve the ambiguity, we are careful to define the grammar so that\n11306| * the decision is staved off as long as possible: as long as we can keep\n11307| * absorbing parentheses into the sub-SELECT, we will do so, and only when\n11308| * it's no longer possible to do that will we decide that parens belong to\n11309| * the expression.\tFor example, in \"SELECT (((SELECT 2)) + 3)\" the extra\n11310| * parentheses are treated as part of the sub-select.  The necessity of doing\n11311| * it that way is shown by \"SELECT (((SELECT 2)) UNION SELECT 2)\".\tHad we\n11312| * parsed \"((SELECT 2))\" as an a_expr, it'd be too late to go back to the\n11313| * SELECT viewpoint when we see the UNION.\n11314| *\n11315| * This approach is implemented by defining a nonterminal select_with_parens,\n11316| * which represents a SELECT with at least one outer layer of parentheses,\n11317| * and being careful to use select_with_parens, never '(' SelectStmt ')',\n11318| * in the expression grammar.  We will then have shift-reduce conflicts\n11319| * which we can resolve in favor of always treating '(' <select> ')' as\n11320| * a select_with_parens.  To resolve the conflicts, the productions that\n11321| * conflict with the select_with_parens productions are manually given\n11322| * precedences lower than the precedence of ')', thereby ensuring that we\n11323| * shift ')' (and then reduce to select_with_parens) rather than trying to\n11324| * reduce the inner <select> nonterminal to something else.  We use UMINUS\n11325| * precedence for this, which is a fairly arbitrary choice.\n11326| *\n11327| * To be able to define select_with_parens itself without ambiguity, we need\n11328| * a nonterminal select_no_parens that represents a SELECT structure with no\n11329| * outermost parentheses.  This is a little bit tedious, but it works.\n11330| *\n11331| * In non-expression contexts, we use SelectStmt which can represent a SELECT\n11332| * with or without outer parentheses.\n11333| */\n11334|\n11335|SelectStmt: select_no_parens\t\t\t%prec UMINUS\n11336|\t\t\t| select_with_parens\t\t%prec UMINUS\n11337|\t\t;\n11338|\n11339|select_with_parens:\n11340|\t\t\t'(' select_no_parens ')'\t\t\t\t{ $$ = $2; }\n11341|\t\t\t| '(' select_with_parens ')'\t\t\t{ $$ = $2; }\n11342|\t\t;\n11343|\n11344|/*\n11345| * This rule parses the equivalent of the standard's <query expression>.\n11346| * The duplicative productions are annoying, but hard to get rid of without\n11347| * creating shift/reduce conflicts.\n11348| *\n11349| *\tThe locking clause (FOR UPDATE etc) may be before or after LIMIT/OFFSET.\n11350| *\tIn <=7.2.X, LIMIT/OFFSET had to be after FOR UPDATE\n11351| *\tWe now support both orderings, but prefer LIMIT/OFFSET before the locking\n11352| * clause.\n11353| *\t2002-08-28 bjm\n11354| */\n11355|select_no_parens:\n11356|\t\t\tsimple_select\t\t\t\t\t\t{ $$ = $1; }\n11357|\t\t\t| select_clause $1;\n11363|\t\t\t\t}\n11364|\t\t\t| select_clause opt_sort_clause for_locking_clause opt_select_limit\n11365|\t\t\t\t{\n11366|\t\t\t\t\tinsertSelectOptions((SelectStmt *) $1, $2, }\n11417|\t\t;\n11418|\n11419|/*\n11420| * This rule parses SELECT statements that can appear within set operations,\n11421| * including UNION, INTERSECT and EXCEPT.  '(' and ')' can be used to specify\n11422| * the ordering of the set operations.\tWithout '(' and ')' we want the\n11423| * operations to be ordered per the precedence specs at the head of this file.\n11424| *\n11425| * As with select_no_parens, simple_select cannot have outer parentheses,\n11426| * but can have parenthesized subclauses.\n11427| *\n11428| * It might appear that we could fold the first two alternatives into one\n11429| * by using opt_distinct_clause.  However, that causes a shift/reduce conflict\n11430| * against INSERT ... SELECT ... ON CONFLICT.  We avoid the ambiguity by\n11431| * requiring SELECT DISTINCT [ON] to be followed by a non-empty target_list.\n11432| *\n11433| * Note that sort clauses cannot be included at this level --- SQL requires\n11434| *\t\tSELECT foo UNION SELECT bar ORDER BY baz\n11435| * to be parsed as\n11436| *\t\t(SELECT foo UNION SELECT bar) ORDER BY baz\n11437| * not\n11438| *\t\tSELECT foo UNION (SELECT bar ORDER BY baz)\n11439| * Likewise for WITH, FOR UPDATE and LIMIT.  Therefore, those clauses are\n11440| * described as part of the select_no_parens production, not simple_select.\n11441| * This does not limit functionality, because you can reintroduce these\n11442| * clauses inside parentheses.\n11443| *\n11444| * NOTE: only the leftmost component SelectStmt should have INTO.\n11445| * However, this is not checked by the grammar; parse analysis must check it.\n11446| */\n11447|simple_select:\n11448|\t\t\tSELECT opt_all_clause opt_target_list\n11449|\t\t\tinto_clause from_clause where_clause\n11450|\t\t\tgroup_clause having_clause *****************************************************************************/\n13231|\n13232|/*\n13233| * General expressions\n13234| * This is the heart of the expression syntax.\n13235| *\n13236| * We have two expression types: a_expr is the unrestricted kind, and\n13237| * b_expr is a subset that must be used in some places to avoid shift/reduce\n13238| * conflicts.  For example, we can't do BETWEEN as \"BETWEEN a_expr AND a_expr\"\n13239| * because that use of AND conflicts with AND as a boolean operator.
